S6 EEE ☀️ Renewable Energy Systems (EET322)
⏱️

3-Hour Exam Time Budget (20 Questions / 100 Marks Strategy)

Part A (10 Short Answers × 3 Marks = 30M): Allocate 5 mins per answer (50 mins total) — target ½ to 1 page highlighting key formula and core definition.
Part B (10 Essay Answers × 7 Marks = 70M): Allocate 12 mins per answer (120 mins total) — target 1.5 to 2 pages with schematic circuit/block diagrams and step-by-step derivations.
Strategy & Review Buffer (10 mins): 5 mins at start for question choice planning + 5 mins at end for question numbering and equation verification.

Course Code: EET322 • 5 Modules • 26 Questions

Renewable Energy Systems

Green power generation and storage: Solar radiation geometry, photovoltaic (PV) cell I-V characteristics & MPPT algorithms (P&O, Incremental Conductance), wind energy conversion systems (Betz limit, HAWT vs VAWT, pitch/yaw control), biomass gasification, small hydro, geothermal & ocean energy, and grid-connected inverter topologies.

Module 1

Solar Radiation Fundamentals & Solar PV Physics

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: RES-Q1

Explain the terms extraterrestrial and terrestrial solar radiation. Define solar constant and air mass ratio with standard values. Explain the terms extraterrestrial and terrestrial solar radiation. Define solar constant and air mass ratio with standard values. Explain the terms extraterrestrial and terrestrial solar radiation. Define solar constant and air mass ratio with standard values.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_{sc} = 1367 \text{ W/m}^2, \quad AM = \frac{1}{\cos\theta_z} = \sec\theta_z, \quad I_{on} = I_{sc}\left[1 + 0.033\cos\left(\frac{360n}{365}\right)\right]$$

Solar Constant: $I_{sc} = 1367\text{ W/m}^2$ is the extraterrestrial solar irradiance normal to the beam at mean earth-sun distance.
Air Mass: $AM = 1/\cos\theta_z$ defines optical atmospheric path length ($AM0$ in space, $AM1.5$ with $1000\text{ W/m}^2$ for terrestrial testing).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_{sc} = 1367 \text{ W/m}^2, \quad AM = \frac{1}{\cos\theta_z} = \sec\theta_z, \quad I_{on} = I_{sc}\left[1 + 0.033\cos\left(\frac{360n}{365}\right)\right]$$

Solar Constant: $I_{sc} = 1367\text{ W/m}^2$ is the extraterrestrial solar irradiance normal to the beam at mean earth-sun distance.
Air Mass: $AM = 1/\cos\theta_z$ defines optical atmospheric path length ($AM0$ in space, $AM1.5$ with $1000\text{ W/m}^2$ for terrestrial testing).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_{sc} = 1367 \text{ W/m}^2, \quad AM = \frac{1}{\cos\theta_z} = \sec\theta_z, \quad I_{on} = I_{sc}\left[1 + 0.033\cos\left(\frac{360n}{365}\right)\right]$$

Solar Constant: $I_{sc} = 1367\text{ W/m}^2$ is the extraterrestrial solar irradiance normal to the beam at mean earth-sun distance.
Air Mass: $AM = 1/\cos\theta_z$ defines optical atmospheric path length ($AM0$ in space, $AM1.5$ with $1000\text{ W/m}^2$ for terrestrial testing).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Extraterrestrial solar radiation represents the electromagnetic energy emitted by the sun that reaches the outer boundary of the earth's atmosphere without experiencing any absorption, scattering, or attenuation. The intensity of this radiation when measured on a surface normal to the sun's rays at the mean earth-sun distance of 1 astronomical unit ($1.496 \times 10^8 \text{ km}$) is termed the Solar Constant, standardly denoted as $I_{sc}$. According to the World Radiation Center (WRC), the internationally accepted value of the solar constant is: $$I_{sc} = 1367 \text{ W/m}^2$$ Because the earth's orbit around the sun is slightly elliptical, the actual extraterrestrial radiation flux $I_{on}$ varies by approximately $\pm 3.3\%$ throughout the year. It is mathematically determined for any day of the year $n$ ($1 \le n \le 365$) using Spencer's formula: $$I_{on} = I_{sc} \left[ 1 + 0.033 \cos\left( \frac{360 n}{365} \right) \right]$$

Terrestrial solar radiation refers to the solar radiation that actually reaches the surface of the earth after traversing through the atmospheric blanket. As extraterrestrial rays propagate through the atmosphere, they undergo substantial attenuation due to Rayleigh scattering by air molecules, Mie scattering by suspended aerosol dust particles and water droplets, and selective spectral absorption by atmospheric gases such as ozone ($O_3$, absorbing ultraviolet rays), water vapor ($H_2 O$), and carbon dioxide ($CO_2$, absorbing infrared bands). Terrestrial radiation comprises two distinct components: Direct or Beam Radiation ($I_b$), which travels in an unscattered optical line of sight directly from the solar disc, and Diffuse Radiation ($I_d$), which arrives at the earth's surface from all directions of the sky dome after multiple scattering events. The sum of both constitutes the Global Horizontal Irradiance ($I_g = I_b \cos\theta_z + I_d$).

The Air Mass ($AM$) ratio is the path length of solar radiation through the earth's atmosphere relative to the shortest possible path length when the sun is directly overhead at the zenith ($\theta_z = 0^\circ$). Under the standard planar atmospheric approximation, Air Mass is given by: $$AM = \frac{\text{Optical path length through atmosphere}}{\text{Path length at zenith}} = \frac{1}{\cos\theta_z} = \sec\theta_z$$ where $\theta_z$ is the solar zenith angle. In space, where no atmosphere exists, the spectrum is designated as $AM0$ ($I_{sc} = 1367 \text{ W/m}^2$), which is used for satellite and spacecraft PV testing. At sea level with the sun directly overhead, $AM = 1.0$. The standard international test condition (STC) for terrestrial solar photovoltaic modules is defined at $AM1.5$, corresponding to a zenith angle of $\theta_z \approx 48.2^\circ$, with an integrated terrestrial irradiance normalized to $1000 \text{ W/m}^2$ at a cell temperature of $25^\circ\text{C}$.

3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: RES-Q2

Define the following solar angles with neat sketches: (i) Declination angle, (ii) Hour angle, (iii) Solar altitude angle, (iv) Zenith angle, and (v) Solar azimuth angle. Define the following solar angles with neat sketches: (i) Declination angle, (ii) Hour angle, (iii) Solar altitude angle, (iv) Zenith angle, and (v) Solar azimuth angle. Define the following solar angles with neat sketches: (i) Declination angle, (ii) Hour angle, (iii) Solar altitude angle, (iv) Zenith angle, and (v) Solar azimuth angle.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\delta = 23.45^\circ \sin\left[\frac{360}{365}(284 + n)\right], \quad \sin\alpha = \cos\theta_z = \sin\phi\sin\delta + \cos\phi\cos\delta\cos\omega$$

Declination ($\delta$): Angle between sun-earth line and equatorial plane ($-23.45^\circ \le \delta \le +23.45^\circ$).
Hour Angle ($\omega$): Earth rotation displacement ($15^\circ/\text{hr}$, $\omega=0^\circ$ at solar noon).
Zenith ($\theta_z$) & Altitude ($\alpha$): $\alpha + \theta_z = 90^\circ$.

⚡ Key Equation

Hour Angle ($\omega$): Earth rotation displacement ($15^\circ/\text{hr}$, $\omega=0^\circ$ at solar noon).
Zenith ($\theta_z$) & Altitude ($\alpha$): $\alpha + \theta_z = 90^\circ$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\delta = 23.45^\circ \sin\left[\frac{360}{365}(284 + n)\right], \quad \sin\alpha = \cos\theta_z = \sin\phi\sin\delta + \cos\phi\cos\delta\cos\omega$$

Declination ($\delta$): Angle between sun-earth line and equatorial plane ($-23.45^\circ \le \delta \le +23.45^\circ$).
Hour Angle ($\omega$): Earth rotation displacement ($15^\circ/\text{hr}$, $\omega=0^\circ$ at solar noon).
Zenith ($\theta_z$) & Altitude ($\alpha$): $\alpha + \theta_z = 90^\circ$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

To precisely design, orient, and tilt solar energy collectors, the instantaneous geometric position of the sun in the celestial sphere relative to the local horizontal plane must be uniquely determined using fundamental angular relationships.

1. Declination Angle ($\delta$): The declination is the angular position of the sun at solar noon relative to the plane of the earth's equator. Because the earth's rotational axis is tilted at an angle of $23.45^\circ$ with respect to its orbital plane around the sun, the declination angle continuously varies between $-23.45^\circ$ at the winter solstice (December 21) and $+23.45^\circ$ at the summer solstice (June 21), passing through $0^\circ$ at the vernal and autumnal equinoxes. For any day of the year $n$ ($1 \le n \le 365$), $\delta$ is determined with high accuracy by Cooper's empirical equation: $$\delta = 23.45^\circ \sin\left[ \frac{360}{365}(284 + n) \right]$$

2. Hour Angle ($\omega$): The hour angle is the angular displacement of the sun east or west of the local celestial meridian due to the earth's rotation on its axis at $15^\circ$ per hour ($360^\circ / 24 \text{ hours}$). By international convention, $\omega = 0^\circ$ at true solar noon. The hour angle is negative in the morning and positive in the afternoon: $$\omega = 15^\circ \times (\text{Solar Time in hours} - 12)$$ For example, at 10:00 AM solar time, $\omega = 15^\circ \times (10 - 12) = -30^\circ$, and at 2:00 PM solar time, $\omega = +30^\circ$.

3. Solar Altitude Angle ($\alpha$) and Zenith Angle ($\theta_z$): The solar altitude angle $\alpha$ is the vertical angular elevation of the sun above the horizontal plane. The solar zenith angle $\theta_z$ is the angle between the sun's rays and the local vertical (zenith direction). Because altitude and zenith are complementary angles: $$\alpha + \theta_z = 90^\circ \implies \theta_z = 90^\circ - \alpha$$ For a location with geographic latitude $\phi$, the altitude angle is computed from the fundamental spherical trigonometry relationship: $$\sin\alpha = \cos\theta_z = \sin\phi \sin\delta + \cos\phi \cos\delta \cos\omega$$

4. Solar Azimuth Angle ($\gamma_s$) and Surface Tilt Angle ($\beta$): The solar azimuth angle $\gamma_s$ is the angular displacement between the true south direction and the projection of the sun's rays on the horizontal plane (negative when east of south, positive when west of south). The collector tilt angle $\beta$ is the angle between the surface of the collector panel and the horizontal plane. For maximum annual solar energy harvest in the northern hemisphere, fixed solar collectors are installed facing due south ($\gamma = 0^\circ$) at a tilt angle approximately equal to the local latitude ($\beta \approx \phi$).

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Descriptive ID: RES-Q3

Explain with neat sketches the construction and working principles of a Pyranometer and a Pyrheliometer for measuring solar radiation. Explain with neat sketches the construction and working principles of a Pyranometer and a Pyrheliometer for measuring solar radiation. Explain with neat sketches the construction and working principles of a Pyranometer and a Pyrheliometer for measuring solar radiation.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{out} = C_{cal} \cdot I_g, \quad I_{bN} = \frac{V_{pyr}}{C_{pyr}}$$

Pyranometer: Measures total hemispherical global radiation ($I_g = I_b\cos\theta_z + I_d$) across $0.3-3.0\,\mu\text{m}$ using a black thermopile with dual glass domes.
Pyrheliometer: Measures beam direct normal irradiance (DNI) using a narrow collimating tube ($5.7^\circ$ field of view) mounted on a 2-axis solar tracker.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{out} = C_{cal} \cdot I_g, \quad I_{bN} = \frac{V_{pyr}}{C_{pyr}}$$

Pyranometer: Measures total hemispherical global radiation ($I_g = I_b\cos\theta_z + I_d$) across $0.3-3.0\,\mu\text{m}$ using a black thermopile with dual glass domes.
Pyrheliometer: Measures beam direct normal irradiance (DNI) using a narrow collimating tube ($5.7^\circ$ field of view) mounted on a 2-axis solar tracker.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{out} = C_{cal} \cdot I_g, \quad I_{bN} = \frac{V_{pyr}}{C_{pyr}}$$

Pyranometer: Measures total hemispherical global radiation ($I_g = I_b\cos\theta_z + I_d$) across $0.3-3.0\,\mu\text{m}$ using a black thermopile with dual glass domes.
Pyrheliometer: Measures beam direct normal irradiance (DNI) using a narrow collimating tube ($5.7^\circ$ field of view) mounted on a 2-axis solar tracker.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Accurate quantitative assessment of solar resource availability requires specialized radiometric instrumentation. The two primary instruments utilized globally in meteorological stations and commercial solar power plants are the Pyranometer and the Pyrheliometer.

1. Pyranometer: A pyranometer is a precision radiometric device designed to measure total global solar radiation (the sum of direct beam and diffuse sky radiation) received from a full hemispherical field of view ($2\pi$ steradians) onto a flat horizontal surface over a broad spectral bandwidth ($0.3 \text{ to } 3.0 \ \mu\text{m}$). Construction and Working Principle: The core sensing element of a standard precision pyranometer is a high-sensitivity multijunction differential thermopile. The active hot junctions of the thermopile are coated with an optical black absorption layer (such as carbon black or black optical paint) having an absorptance exceeding $0.97$ across the entire solar spectrum. The reference cold junctions are thermally coupled to a massive, polished brass or aluminum instrument heat sink protected under a radiation shield. When sunlight impinges on the black receiver, it absorbs radiant energy, creating a sharp temperature differential $\Delta T$ between the hot and cold junctions. By the Seebeck thermoelectric effect, this temperature gradient induces a minute DC voltage proportional to the incident radiant flux: $$V_{out} = C_{cal} \cdot I_g$$ where $C_{cal}$ is the factory calibration sensitivity coefficient (typically $5 \text{ to } 10 \ \mu\text{V} / (\text{W/m}^2)$) and $I_g$ is the global irradiance in $\text{W/m}^2$. To isolate the thermopile sensor from convective wind cooling and ambient weather perturbations, the sensor is sealed beneath two concentric hemispherical optical glass domes made of high-purity optical grade Schott glass. The outer dome reflects long-wave thermal radiation, and the inner dome provides a stagnant air insulating barrier. A silica-gel desiccant chamber prevents internal moisture condensation. Diffuse Radiation Measurement: A pyranometer can also measure purely diffuse solar radiation ($I_d$) by shielding the sensor disc from the direct solar beam using either an automated shadow band or an motorized sun-tracking occulting disc.

2. Pyrheliometer: A pyrheliometer is a directional radiometric instrument designed exclusively to measure Beam or Direct Normal Irradiance (DNI), which is the solar flux coming directly from the solar disc without sky-diffuse components, received on a surface held perpendicular to the sun's rays. Construction and Working Principle: The sensor consists of a black thermopile detector positioned at the bottom base of an elongated collimating tube. The collimating tube has a precisely machined aperture at its entrance that limits the field of view to a narrow full acceptance angle of exactly $5.7^\circ$ (or approximately $5^\circ$ slope angle per ISO 9060 standards). The inside of the collimating tube is blackened and baffled with a succession of knife-edge circular diaphragms to suppress internal optical reflections and eliminate stray diffuse light. Because the acceptance angle is extremely narrow, the pyrheliometer must constantly point directly at the moving sun throughout the day. It is mounted on an automated dual-axis celestial sun tracker driven by microprocessor-controlled stepper motors and sun-position ephemeris algorithms. The thermopile generates a thermoelectric voltage directly proportional to the direct normal irradiance: $$I_{bN} = \frac{V_{pyr}}{C_{pyr}}$$ Pyrheliometer measurements are indispensable for Concentrated Solar Power (CSP) plants, such as parabolic trough and solar tower systems, which can concentrate only parallel direct rays and cannot utilize diffuse radiation.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Accurate quantitative assessment of solar resource availability requires specialized radiometric instrumentation. The two primary instruments utilized globally in meteorological stations and commercial solar power plants are the Pyranometer and the Pyrheliometer.

1. Pyranometer: A pyranometer is a precision radiometric device designed to measure total global solar radiation (the sum of direct beam and diffuse sky radiation) received from a full hemispherical field of view ($2\pi$ steradians) onto a flat horizontal surface over a broad spectral bandwidth ($0.3 \text{ to } 3.0 \ \mu\text{m}$). Construction and Working Principle: The core sensing element of a standard precision pyranometer is a high-sensitivity multijunction differential thermopile. The active hot junctions of the thermopile are coated with an optical black absorption layer (such as carbon black or black optical paint) having an absorptance exceeding $0.97$ across the entire solar spectrum. The reference cold junctions are thermally coupled to a massive, polished brass or aluminum instrument heat sink protected under a radiation shield. When sunlight impinges on the black receiver, it absorbs radiant energy, creating a sharp temperature differential $\Delta T$ between the hot and cold junctions. By the Seebeck thermoelectric effect, this temperature gradient induces a minute DC voltage proportional to the incident radiant flux: $$V_{out} = C_{cal} \cdot I_g$$ where $C_{cal}$ is the factory calibration sensitivity coefficient (typically $5 \text{ to } 10 \ \mu\text{V} / (\text{W/m}^2)$) and $I_g$ is the global irradiance in $\text{W/m}^2$. To isolate the thermopile sensor from convective wind cooling and ambient weather perturbations, the sensor is sealed beneath two concentric hemispherical optical glass domes made of high-purity optical grade Schott glass. The outer dome reflects long-wave thermal radiation, and the inner dome provides a stagnant air insulating barrier. A silica-gel desiccant chamber prevents internal moisture condensation. Diffuse Radiation Measurement: A pyranometer can also measure purely diffuse solar radiation ($I_d$) by shielding the sensor disc from the direct solar beam using either an automated shadow band or an motorized sun-tracking occulting disc.

2. Pyrheliometer: A pyrheliometer is a directional radiometric instrument designed exclusively to measure Beam or Direct Normal Irradiance (DNI), which is the solar flux coming directly from the solar disc without sky-diffuse components, received on a surface held perpendicular to the sun's rays. Construction and Working Principle: The sensor consists of a black thermopile detector positioned at the bottom base of an elongated collimating tube. The collimating tube has a precisely machined aperture at its entrance that limits the field of view to a narrow full acceptance angle of exactly $5.7^\circ$ (or approximately $5^\circ$ slope angle per ISO 9060 standards). The inside of the collimating tube is blackened and baffled with a succession of knife-edge circular diaphragms to suppress internal optical reflections and eliminate stray diffuse light. Because the acceptance angle is extremely narrow, the pyrheliometer must constantly point directly at the moving sun throughout the day. It is mounted on an automated dual-axis celestial sun tracker driven by microprocessor-controlled stepper motors and sun-position ephemeris algorithms. The thermopile generates a thermoelectric voltage directly proportional to the direct normal irradiance: $$I_{bN} = \frac{V_{pyr}}{C_{pyr}}$$ Pyrheliometer measurements are indispensable for Concentrated Solar Power (CSP) plants, such as parabolic trough and solar tower systems, which can concentrate only parallel direct rays and cannot utilize diffuse radiation.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: RES-Q4

Derive the single-diode equivalent circuit model and the governing I-V characteristic equation of a practical solar photovoltaic cell. Derive the single-diode equivalent circuit model and the governing I-V characteristic equation of a practical solar photovoltaic cell. Derive the single-diode equivalent circuit model and the governing I-V characteristic equation of a practical solar photovoltaic cell.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I = I_{ph} - I_0 \left[ \exp\left( \frac{q (V + I R_s)}{n k T} \right) - 1 \right] - \frac{V + I R_s}{R_{sh}}$$

Single-diode PV equivalent circuit couples light-generated current $I_{ph}$, dark diode recombination current $I_D$, series contact resistance $R_s$, and parallel shunt leakage resistance $R_{sh}$. Open-circuit voltage scales logarithmically: $V_{oc} \approx V_t \cdot n \ln(I_{sc}/I_0)$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I = I_{ph} - I_0 \left[ \exp\left( \frac{q (V + I R_s)}{n k T} \right) - 1 \right] - \frac{V + I R_s}{R_{sh}}$$

Single-diode PV equivalent circuit couples light-generated current $I_{ph}$, dark diode recombination current $I_D$, series contact resistance $R_s$, and parallel shunt leakage resistance $R_{sh}$. Open-circuit voltage scales logarithmically: $V_{oc} \approx V_t \cdot n \ln(I_{sc}/I_0)$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I = I_{ph} - I_0 \left[ \exp\left( \frac{q (V + I R_s)}{n k T} \right) - 1 \right] - \frac{V + I R_s}{R_{sh}}$$

Single-diode PV equivalent circuit couples light-generated current $I_{ph}$, dark diode recombination current $I_D$, series contact resistance $R_s$, and parallel shunt leakage resistance $R_{sh}$. Open-circuit voltage scales logarithmically: $V_{oc} \approx V_t \cdot n \ln(I_{sc}/I_0)$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A solar photovoltaic cell is essentially a large-area semiconductor p-n junction diode optimized for the conversion of incident photon energy directly into electrical energy through the photovoltaic effect. When incident photons have an energy $E_{ph} = h\nu$ greater than or equal to the semiconductor bandgap energy $E_g$ ($E_g \approx 1.12 \text{ eV}$ for crystalline silicon at $300\text{ K}$), photons are absorbed, exciting electrons from the valence band into the conduction band, thereby generating electron-hole pairs (EHPs).

Within the built-in space-charge depletion region, an internal electric field exists directed from the donor-doped n-side to the acceptor-doped p-side. This strong electrostatic field immediately separates the generated carrier pairs before they can recombine, sweeping the minority electrons into the n-region and minority holes into the p-region. This spatial charge accumulation creates an open-circuit photovoltage across the external terminals and drives a continuous photocurrent when connected to an external load.

To analyze the electrical behavior of a real solar cell, we construct its standard single-diode equivalent circuit model. The practical equivalent circuit consists of: 1. An ideal constant current source producing light-generated photocurrent $I_{ph}$ proportional to solar irradiance. 2. A parallel semiconductor p-n junction diode modeling dark recombination currents. 3. A parallel shunt resistance $R_{sh}$ representing leakage currents across the p-n junction edges and structural crystal defects. 4. A series resistance $R_s$ representing the ohmic contact resistance of metallic grid fingers, busbars, and bulk semiconductor material.

Applying Kirchhoff's Current Law (KCL) at the top output node: $$I = I_{ph} - I_D - I_{sh}$$ The current flowing through the parallel diode is governed by Shockley's ideal diode equation: $$I_D = I_0 \left[ \exp\left( \frac{q V_D}{n k T} \right) - 1 \right]$$ where: - $I_0$ is the reverse saturation (dark) current of the diode, - $q$ is the electronic charge ($1.602 \times 10^{-19} \text{ C}$), - $k$ is the Boltzmann constant ($1.381 \times 10^{-23} \text{ J/K}$), - $T$ is the absolute junction temperature in Kelvin, - $n$ is the diode ideality factor (typically $1.0 \le n \le 2.0$), - $V_t = \frac{k T}{q}$ is the thermal voltage ($V_t \approx 0.0259 \text{ V}$ at $25^\circ\text{C} = 298.15\text{ K}$).

From the equivalent circuit loop, the voltage across the diode $V_D$ is related to terminal voltage $V$ and terminal current $I$ by: $$V_D = V + I R_s$$ The leakage current diverted through the shunt resistance is: $$I_{sh} = \frac{V_D}{R_{sh}} = \frac{V + I R_s}{R_{sh}}$$

Substituting the expressions for $I_D$ and $I_{sh}$ into the KCL node equation yields the celebrated transcendental characteristic equation of a practical solar cell: $$I = I_{ph} - I_0 \left[ \exp\left( \frac{q (V + I R_s)}{n k T} \right) - 1 \right] - \frac{V + I R_s}{R_{sh}}$$

For an ideal solar cell, parasitic losses are neglected ($R_s = 0$ and $R_{sh} \to \infty$). Under these ideal conditions, the equation reduces to: $$I = I_{ph} - I_0 \left[ \exp\left( \frac{q V}{n k T} \right) - 1 \right]$$ Setting $V = 0$ yields the short-circuit current: $$I_{sc} \approx I_{ph}$$ Setting $I = 0$ yields the open-circuit voltage: $$0 = I_{sc} - I_0 \left[ \exp\left( \frac{q V_{oc}}{n k T} \right) - 1 \right]$$ Since $\exp\left( \frac{q V_{oc}}{n k T} \right) \gg 1$: $$V_{oc} = \frac{n k T}{q} \ln\left( \frac{I_{sc}}{I_0} + 1 \right) \approx V_t \cdot n \ln\left( \frac{I_{sc}}{I_0} \right)$$ This confirms that $I_{sc}$ increases linearly with incident solar irradiance, whereas $V_{oc}$ increases logarithmically with irradiance and decreases significantly with increasing cell operating temperature.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Numerical Problem ID: RES-Q5

Define Open-Circuit Voltage, Short-Circuit Current, Maximum Power Point, Fill Factor, and Conversion Efficiency of a PV cell with step-by-step numerical calculation. Define Open-Circuit Voltage, Short-Circuit Current, Maximum Power Point, Fill Factor, and Conversion Efficiency of a PV cell with step-by-step numerical calculation. Define Open-Circuit Voltage, Short-Circuit Current, Maximum Power Point, Fill Factor, and Conversion Efficiency of a PV cell with step-by-step numerical calculation.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$FF = \frac{P_{mp}}{V_{oc} I_{sc}} = \frac{V_{mp} I_{mp}}{V_{oc} I_{sc}}, \quad \eta = \frac{P_{mp}}{G \cdot A} \times 100\% = \frac{V_{oc} I_{sc} FF}{G \cdot A} \times 100\%$$

Fill Factor ($FF$): Measures the squareness of the I-V curve (typically $0.75 - 0.85$).
Conversion Efficiency ($\eta$): Ratio of maximum electrical power $P_{mp}$ to total incident solar optical power $P_{in} = G \cdot A$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$FF = \frac{P_{mp}}{V_{oc} I_{sc}} = \frac{V_{mp} I_{mp}}{V_{oc} I_{sc}}, \quad \eta = \frac{P_{mp}}{G \cdot A} \times 100\% = \frac{V_{oc} I_{sc} FF}{G \cdot A} \times 100\%$$

Fill Factor ($FF$): Measures the squareness of the I-V curve (typically $0.75 - 0.85$).
Conversion Efficiency ($\eta$): Ratio of maximum electrical power $P_{mp}$ to total incident solar optical power $P_{in} = G \cdot A$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$FF = \frac{P_{mp}}{V_{oc} I_{sc}} = \frac{V_{mp} I_{mp}}{V_{oc} I_{sc}}, \quad \eta = \frac{P_{mp}}{G \cdot A} \times 100\% = \frac{V_{oc} I_{sc} FF}{G \cdot A} \times 100\%$$

Fill Factor ($FF$): Measures the squareness of the I-V curve (typically $0.75 - 0.85$).
Conversion Efficiency ($\eta$): Ratio of maximum electrical power $P_{mp}$ to total incident solar optical power $P_{in} = G \cdot A$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The performance of a photovoltaic device is evaluated through its Current-Voltage ($I-V$) and Power-Voltage ($P-V$) characteristic curves. Four critical parameters define the operating limits:

1. Short-Circuit Current ($I_{sc}$): The maximum current delivered by the cell when the external load impedance is zero ($V = 0$). For an ideal cell, $I_{sc} = I_{ph}$. 2. Open-Circuit Voltage ($V_{oc}$): The maximum terminal voltage developed when the external load is open-circuited ($I = 0$). 3. Maximum Power Point ($P_{mp}$): The unique point on the $I-V$ curve where the product of terminal voltage $V$ and current $I$ attains its absolute maximum: $$P_{mp} = V_{mp} \cdot I_{mp}$$ At this point, $\frac{dP}{dV} = 0$, which yields $\frac{d(VI)}{dV} = I + V \frac{dI}{dV} = 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} = -\frac{I}{V}$. 4. Fill Factor ($FF$): A dimensionless measure of the squareness of the $I-V$ curve, defined as the ratio of maximum harvestable power to the product of $V_{oc}$ and $I_{sc}$: $$FF = \frac{P_{mp}}{V_{oc} \cdot I_{sc}} = \frac{V_{mp} \cdot I_{mp}}{V_{oc} \cdot I_{sc}}$$ For high-quality commercial silicon solar cells, $FF$ ranges between $0.75$ and $0.85$ ($75\%$ to $85\%$). Higher series resistance or lower shunt resistance severely degrades the fill factor. 5. Conversion Efficiency ($\eta$): The ratio of maximum electrical power output $P_{mp}$ to total incident solar optical power $P_{in}$ over cell surface area $A$: $$\eta = \frac{P_{mp}}{P_{in}} \times 100\% = \frac{V_{mp} I_{mp}}{G \cdot A} \times 100\% = \frac{V_{oc} \cdot I_{sc} \cdot FF}{G \cdot A} \times 100\%$$ where $G$ is the incident solar irradiance ($\text{W/m}^2$) and $A$ is the aperture surface area of the cell ($\text{m}^2$).

--- NUMERICAL PROBLEM (KTU Model Examination): A monocrystalline silicon solar cell of dimensions $12.5\text{ cm} \times 12.5\text{ cm}$ is subjected to standard test conditions (STC: irradiance $G = 1000\text{ W/m}^2$ at temperature $T = 25^\circ\text{C}$). Under these conditions, the cell exhibits: - Short-circuit current $I_{sc} = 5.25\text{ A}$ - Open-circuit voltage $V_{oc} = 0.62\text{ V}$ - Voltage at maximum power $V_{mp} = 0.51\text{ V}$ - Current at maximum power $I_{mp} = 4.80\text{ A}$ Determine: (a) The cell active surface area $A$ and incident solar power $P_{in}$. (b) The maximum electrical power output $P_{mp}$. (c) The Fill Factor ($FF$). (d) The solar energy conversion efficiency ($\eta$).

SOLUTION STEP-BY-STEP: Step 1: Calculate the cell surface area and total incident power: $$A = 12.5\text{ cm} \times 12.5\text{ cm} = 0.125\text{ m} \times 0.125\text{ m} = 0.015625\text{ m}^2$$ The total incident radiant optical power is: $$P_{in} = G \times A = 1000\text{ W/m}^2 \times 0.015625\text{ m}^2 = 15.625\text{ W}$$

Step 2: Calculate the maximum electrical power output: $$P_{mp} = V_{mp} \times I_{mp} = 0.51\text{ V} \times 4.80\text{ A} = 2.448\text{ W}$$

Step 3: Calculate the Fill Factor: The nominal product of open-circuit voltage and short-circuit current is: $$V_{oc} \times I_{sc} = 0.62\text{ V} \times 5.25\text{ A} = 3.255\text{ W}$$ Applying the Fill Factor formula: $$FF = \frac{P_{mp}}{V_{oc} \times I_{sc}} = \frac{2.448}{3.255} \approx 0.7521 \implies FF = 75.21\%$$

Step 4: Calculate the solar cell conversion efficiency: $$\eta = \frac{P_{mp}}{P_{in}} \times 100\% = \frac{2.448\text{ W}}{15.625\text{ W}} \times 100\% = 15.6672\% \approx 15.67\%$$ Final highlighted results: $$\mathbf{P_{mp} = 2.448\text{ W}}, \quad \mathbf{FF = 0.7521 \ (75.21\%)}, \quad \mathbf{\eta = 15.67\%}$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The performance of a photovoltaic device is evaluated through its Current-Voltage ($I-V$) and Power-Voltage ($P-V$) characteristic curves. Four critical parameters define the operating limits:

1. Short-Circuit Current ($I_{sc}$): The maximum current delivered by the cell when the external load impedance is zero ($V = 0$). For an ideal cell, $I_{sc} = I_{ph}$. 2. Open-Circuit Voltage ($V_{oc}$): The maximum terminal voltage developed when the external load is open-circuited ($I = 0$). 3. Maximum Power Point ($P_{mp}$): The unique point on the $I-V$ curve where the product of terminal voltage $V$ and current $I$ attains its absolute maximum: $$P_{mp} = V_{mp} \cdot I_{mp}$$ At this point, $\frac{dP}{dV} = 0$, which yields $\frac{d(VI)}{dV} = I + V \frac{dI}{dV} = 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} = -\frac{I}{V}$. 4. Fill Factor ($FF$): A dimensionless measure of the squareness of the $I-V$ curve, defined as the ratio of maximum harvestable power to the product of $V_{oc}$ and $I_{sc}$: $$FF = \frac{P_{mp}}{V_{oc} \cdot I_{sc}} = \frac{V_{mp} \cdot I_{mp}}{V_{oc} \cdot I_{sc}}$$ For high-quality commercial silicon solar cells, $FF$ ranges between $0.75$ and $0.85$ ($75\%$ to $85\%$). Higher series resistance or lower shunt resistance severely degrades the fill factor. 5. Conversion Efficiency ($\eta$): The ratio of maximum electrical power output $P_{mp}$ to total incident solar optical power $P_{in}$ over cell surface area $A$: $$\eta = \frac{P_{mp}}{P_{in}} \times 100\% = \frac{V_{mp} I_{mp}}{G \cdot A} \times 100\% = \frac{V_{oc} \cdot I_{sc} \cdot FF}{G \cdot A} \times 100\%$$ where $G$ is the incident solar irradiance ($\text{W/m}^2$) and $A$ is the aperture surface area of the cell ($\text{m}^2$).

--- NUMERICAL PROBLEM (KTU Model Examination): A monocrystalline silicon solar cell of dimensions $12.5\text{ cm} \times 12.5\text{ cm}$ is subjected to standard test conditions (STC: irradiance $G = 1000\text{ W/m}^2$ at temperature $T = 25^\circ\text{C}$). Under these conditions, the cell exhibits: - Short-circuit current $I_{sc} = 5.25\text{ A}$ - Open-circuit voltage $V_{oc} = 0.62\text{ V}$ - Voltage at maximum power $V_{mp} = 0.51\text{ V}$ - Current at maximum power $I_{mp} = 4.80\text{ A}$ Determine: (a) The cell active surface area $A$ and incident solar power $P_{in}$. (b) The maximum electrical power output $P_{mp}$. (c) The Fill Factor ($FF$). (d) The solar energy conversion efficiency ($\eta$).

SOLUTION STEP-BY-STEP: Step 1: Calculate the cell surface area and total incident power: $$A = 12.5\text{ cm} \times 12.5\text{ cm} = 0.125\text{ m} \times 0.125\text{ m} = 0.015625\text{ m}^2$$ The total incident radiant optical power is: $$P_{in} = G \times A = 1000\text{ W/m}^2 \times 0.015625\text{ m}^2 = 15.625\text{ W}$$

Step 2: Calculate the maximum electrical power output: $$P_{mp} = V_{mp} \times I_{mp} = 0.51\text{ V} \times 4.80\text{ A} = 2.448\text{ W}$$

Step 3: Calculate the Fill Factor: The nominal product of open-circuit voltage and short-circuit current is: $$V_{oc} \times I_{sc} = 0.62\text{ V} \times 5.25\text{ A} = 3.255\text{ W}$$ Applying the Fill Factor formula: $$FF = \frac{P_{mp}}{V_{oc} \times I_{sc}} = \frac{2.448}{3.255} \approx 0.7521 \implies FF = 75.21\%$$

Step 4: Calculate the solar cell conversion efficiency: $$\eta = \frac{P_{mp}}{P_{in}} \times 100\% = \frac{2.448\text{ W}}{15.625\text{ W}} \times 100\% = 15.6672\% \approx 15.67\%$$ Final highlighted results: $$\mathbf{P_{mp} = 2.448\text{ W}}, \quad \mathbf{FF = 0.7521 \ (75.21\%)}, \quad \mathbf{\eta = 15.67\%}$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

The performance of a photovoltaic device is evaluated through its Current-Voltage ($I-V$) and Power-Voltage ($P-V$) characteristic curves. Four critical parameters define the operating limits:

1. Short-Circuit Current ($I_{sc}$): The maximum current delivered by the cell when the external load impedance is zero ($V = 0$). For an ideal cell, $I_{sc} = I_{ph}$. 2. Open-Circuit Voltage ($V_{oc}$): The maximum terminal voltage developed when the external load is open-circuited ($I = 0$). 3. Maximum Power Point ($P_{mp}$): The unique point on the $I-V$ curve where the product of terminal voltage $V$ and current $I$ attains its absolute maximum: $$P_{mp} = V_{mp} \cdot I_{mp}$$ At this point, $\frac{dP}{dV} = 0$, which yields $\frac{d(VI)}{dV} = I + V \frac{dI}{dV} = 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} = -\frac{I}{V}$. 4. Fill Factor ($FF$): A dimensionless measure of the squareness of the $I-V$ curve, defined as the ratio of maximum harvestable power to the product of $V_{oc}$ and $I_{sc}$: $$FF = \frac{P_{mp}}{V_{oc} \cdot I_{sc}} = \frac{V_{mp} \cdot I_{mp}}{V_{oc} \cdot I_{sc}}$$ For high-quality commercial silicon solar cells, $FF$ ranges between $0.75$ and $0.85$ ($75\%$ to $85\%$). Higher series resistance or lower shunt resistance severely degrades the fill factor. 5. Conversion Efficiency ($\eta$): The ratio of maximum electrical power output $P_{mp}$ to total incident solar optical power $P_{in}$ over cell surface area $A$: $$\eta = \frac{P_{mp}}{P_{in}} \times 100\% = \frac{V_{mp} I_{mp}}{G \cdot A} \times 100\% = \frac{V_{oc} \cdot I_{sc} \cdot FF}{G \cdot A} \times 100\%$$ where $G$ is the incident solar irradiance ($\text{W/m}^2$) and $A$ is the aperture surface area of the cell ($\text{m}^2$).

--- NUMERICAL PROBLEM (KTU Model Examination): A monocrystalline silicon solar cell of dimensions $12.5\text{ cm} \times 12.5\text{ cm}$ is subjected to standard test conditions (STC: irradiance $G = 1000\text{ W/m}^2$ at temperature $T = 25^\circ\text{C}$). Under these conditions, the cell exhibits: - Short-circuit current $I_{sc} = 5.25\text{ A}$ - Open-circuit voltage $V_{oc} = 0.62\text{ V}$ - Voltage at maximum power $V_{mp} = 0.51\text{ V}$ - Current at maximum power $I_{mp} = 4.80\text{ A}$ Determine: (a) The cell active surface area $A$ and incident solar power $P_{in}$. (b) The maximum electrical power output $P_{mp}$. (c) The Fill Factor ($FF$). (d) The solar energy conversion efficiency ($\eta$).

SOLUTION STEP-BY-STEP: Step 1: Calculate the cell surface area and total incident power: $$A = 12.5\text{ cm} \times 12.5\text{ cm} = 0.125\text{ m} \times 0.125\text{ m} = 0.015625\text{ m}^2$$ The total incident radiant optical power is: $$P_{in} = G \times A = 1000\text{ W/m}^2 \times 0.015625\text{ m}^2 = 15.625\text{ W}$$

Step 2: Calculate the maximum electrical power output: $$P_{mp} = V_{mp} \times I_{mp} = 0.51\text{ V} \times 4.80\text{ A} = 2.448\text{ W}$$

Step 3: Calculate the Fill Factor: The nominal product of open-circuit voltage and short-circuit current is: $$V_{oc} \times I_{sc} = 0.62\text{ V} \times 5.25\text{ A} = 3.255\text{ W}$$ Applying the Fill Factor formula: $$FF = \frac{P_{mp}}{V_{oc} \times I_{sc}} = \frac{2.448}{3.255} \approx 0.7521 \implies FF = 75.21\%$$

Step 4: Calculate the solar cell conversion efficiency: $$\eta = \frac{P_{mp}}{P_{in}} \times 100\% = \frac{2.448\text{ W}}{15.625\text{ W}} \times 100\% = 15.6672\% \approx 15.67\%$$ Final highlighted results: $$\mathbf{P_{mp} = 2.448\text{ W}}, \quad \mathbf{FF = 0.7521 \ (75.21\%)}, \quad \mathbf{\eta = 15.67\%}$$

Solar Thermal Collectors & Solar PV Systems

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: RES-Q6

Explain the construction and working of a liquid flat-plate solar collector. Derive the Hottel-Whillier-Bliss useful energy balance equation and efficiency. Explain the construction and working of a liquid flat-plate solar collector. Derive the Hottel-Whillier-Bliss useful energy balance equation and efficiency. Explain the construction and working of a liquid flat-plate solar collector. Derive the Hottel-Whillier-Bliss useful energy balance equation and efficiency.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$q_u = F_R A_c [I_t (\tau\alpha) - U_L (T_i - T_a)], \quad \eta = F_R (\tau\alpha) - F_R U_L \left(\frac{T_i - T_a}{I_t}\right)$$

Hottel-Whillier-Bliss Equation: Quantifies useful thermal gain $q_u$ of a flat-plate collector using heat removal factor $F_R$, optical transmittance-absorptance product $(\tau\alpha)$, and overall heat loss coefficient $U_L$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$q_u = F_R A_c [I_t (\tau\alpha) - U_L (T_i - T_a)], \quad \eta = F_R (\tau\alpha) - F_R U_L \left(\frac{T_i - T_a}{I_t}\right)$$

Hottel-Whillier-Bliss Equation: Quantifies useful thermal gain $q_u$ of a flat-plate collector using heat removal factor $F_R$, optical transmittance-absorptance product $(\tau\alpha)$, and overall heat loss coefficient $U_L$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$q_u = F_R A_c [I_t (\tau\alpha) - U_L (T_i - T_a)], \quad \eta = F_R (\tau\alpha) - F_R U_L \left(\frac{T_i - T_a}{I_t}\right)$$

Hottel-Whillier-Bliss Equation: Quantifies useful thermal gain $q_u$ of a flat-plate collector using heat removal factor $F_R$, optical transmittance-absorptance product $(\tau\alpha)$, and overall heat loss coefficient $U_L$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A liquid flat-plate solar collector (FPC) is a non-concentrating solar thermal device designed to absorb incident solar radiation and transfer the collected thermal energy to a working liquid (typically water or a water-glycol mixture) flowing through embedded tubes for domestic hot water, space heating, and low-temperature industrial processes ($50^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 90^\circ\text{C}$).

Constructional Components: 1. Absorber Plate: A high-conductivity metallic sheet (copper or aluminum) coated with a spectrally selective surface having high solar absorptance ($\alpha \ge 0.95$) in the short-wave solar spectrum and low thermal emittance ($\varepsilon \le 0.10$) in the long-wave infrared spectrum (e.g., black chrome or titanium nitrite oxide). 2. Riser Tubes and Headers: A grid of parallel copper riser tubes brazed or laser-welded onto the absorber plate, fed by lower and upper fluid distribution headers. 3. Transparent Glazing: One or two sheets of low-iron tempered glass ($3.2 \text{ to } 4\text{ mm}$ thick) with high transmittance ($\tau \approx 0.90$) that admit shortwave solar rays while blocking long-wave thermal radiation emitted by the hot absorber plate (the greenhouse effect). 4. Thermal Insulation: Mineral wool, polyurethane foam, or fiberglass layers placed beneath the bottom and around the sidewalls to suppress conductive heat loss. 5. Protective Casing: A weatherproof aluminum or galvanized sheet steel casing that seals the assembly against wind, rain, and dust.

Derivation of Hottel-Whillier-Bliss Energy Balance Equation: Under steady-state conditions, the incident solar energy absorbed by the collector plate of aperture area $A_c$ is: $$q_{absorbed} = A_c \cdot S = A_c \left[ I_t (\tau\alpha) \right]$$ where $I_t$ is the total incident solar irradiance on the tilted collector aperture, and $(\tau\alpha)$ is the effective transmittance-absorptance product of the cover-plate system.

The absorber plate operates at a mean temperature $T_p$ higher than the ambient temperature $T_a$, leading to thermal losses back to the environment via radiation, convection, and conduction. These combined heat losses are expressed using an overall heat loss coefficient $U_L$: $$q_{loss} = A_c \cdot U_L (T_p - T_a)$$ where $U_L = U_{top} + U_{bottom} + U_{edge}$. Thus, the net useful thermal power extracted by the collector is: $$q_u = q_{absorbed} - q_{loss} = A_c \left[ S - U_L (T_p - T_a) \right]$$

Because the actual mean plate temperature $T_p$ is difficult to measure experimentally across the entire plate surface, Hottel, Whillier, and Bliss introduced the Collector Heat Removal Factor $F_R$. $F_R$ is a dimensionless factor defined as the ratio of actual useful heat gain to the maximum possible heat gain that would occur if the entire absorber plate were at the fluid inlet temperature $T_i$: $$q_u = F_R A_c \left[ S - U_L (T_i - T_a) \right] = F_R A_c \left[ I_t (\tau\alpha) - U_L (T_i - T_a) \right]$$ The useful heat gain can also be determined from the fluid mass flow rate $\dot{m}$, fluid specific heat capacity $C_p$, and temperature rise between inlet $T_i$ and outlet $T_o$: $$q_u = \dot{m} C_p (T_o - T_i)$$

Equating both expressions yields the celebrated Hottel-Whillier-Bliss instantaneous collector thermal efficiency $\eta$: $$\eta = \frac{q_u}{A_c I_t} = \frac{F_R A_c \left[ I_t (\tau\alpha) - U_L (T_i - T_a) \right]}{A_c I_t}$$ $$\eta = F_R (\tau\alpha) - F_R U_L \left( \frac{T_i - T_a}{I_t} \right)$$ Plotting $\eta$ against the reduced temperature parameter $\left( \frac{T_i - T_a}{I_t} \right)$ produces a straight line where: - The y-intercept represents the optical efficiency $F_R (\tau\alpha)$, - The negative slope represents the thermal loss parameter $-F_R U_L$.

A liquid flat-plate solar collector (FPC) is a non-concentrating solar thermal device designed to absorb incident solar radiation and transfer the collected thermal energy to a working liquid (typically water or a water-glycol mixture) flowing through embedded tubes for domestic hot water, space heating, and low-temperature industrial processes ($50^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 90^\circ\text{C}$).

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Descriptive ID: RES-Q7

Classify concentrating solar thermal collectors. Compare Parabolic Trough, Linear Fresnel, Solar Power Tower, and Parabolic Dish systems. Classify concentrating solar thermal collectors. Compare Parabolic Trough, Linear Fresnel, Solar Power Tower, and Parabolic Dish systems. Classify concentrating solar thermal collectors. Compare Parabolic Trough, Linear Fresnel, Solar Power Tower, and Parabolic Dish systems.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$C_R = \frac{A_a}{A_r}, \quad PTC: C_R \approx 15-80, \quad CR (\text{Tower}): C_R \approx 500-1500$$

Concentrating Solar Power (CSP) reduces heat loss area $A_r$ relative to aperture $A_a$ to achieve high operating temperatures.
Line Focus: Parabolic Trough ($400^\circ\text{C}$) & Linear Fresnel ($350^\circ\text{C}$).
Point Focus: Solar Power Tower ($550-800^\circ\text{C}$) with molten salt storage.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$C_R = \frac{A_a}{A_r}, \quad PTC: C_R \approx 15-80, \quad CR (\text{Tower}): C_R \approx 500-1500$$

Concentrating Solar Power (CSP) reduces heat loss area $A_r$ relative to aperture $A_a$ to achieve high operating temperatures.
Line Focus: Parabolic Trough ($400^\circ\text{C}$) & Linear Fresnel ($350^\circ\text{C}$).
Point Focus: Solar Power Tower ($550-800^\circ\text{C}$) with molten salt storage.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$C_R = \frac{A_a}{A_r}, \quad PTC: C_R \approx 15-80, \quad CR (\text{Tower}): C_R \approx 500-1500$$

Concentrating Solar Power (CSP) reduces heat loss area $A_r$ relative to aperture $A_a$ to achieve high operating temperatures.
Line Focus: Parabolic Trough ($400^\circ\text{C}$) & Linear Fresnel ($350^\circ\text{C}$).
Point Focus: Solar Power Tower ($550-800^\circ\text{C}$) with molten salt storage.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Concentrating Solar Power (CSP) systems utilize optical reflectors (mirrors) to concentrate direct normal solar irradiance (DNI) from a large aperture area onto a significantly smaller absorber target. Concentrating the solar flux achieves elevated working temperatures ($300^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 1000^\circ\text{C}+$ ), driving conventional steam or gas turbine thermal cycles at high thermodynamic efficiency.

Concentration Ratio ($C_R$): The fundamental figure of merit is the geometric concentration ratio $C_R$, defined as: $$C_R = \frac{\text{Aperture Area of Concentrator } (A_a)}{\text{Surface Area of Receiver Absorber } (A_r)}$$ As $C_R$ increases, the thermal loss area $A_r$ shrinks relative to the collecting aperture $A_a$, enabling much higher operating temperatures without excessive radiation heat loss.

1. Parabolic Trough Collector (PTC) - Line Focus: - Geometry & Optics: PTC consists of long, U-shaped parabolic curved mirrors that focus direct sunlight onto a linear metallic absorber tube positioned along the focal line. It utilizes single-axis tracking (typically tracking from East to West). - Receiver: A stainless steel pipe coated with cermet selective coating surrounded by an evacuated glass envelope to eliminate convection losses. Synthetic thermal oil or molten salt circulates through the tube. - Operating Temperature: $350^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 400^\circ\text{C}$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 15 \text{ to } 80$. - Status: The most mature and widely deployed CSP technology globally (e.g., SEGS and Mojave plants).

2. Linear Fresnel Reflector (LFR) - Line Focus: - Geometry & Optics: Instead of curved parabolic mirrors, LFR employs long, flat or slightly curved elastic mirror strips placed close to the ground that rotate independently to focus sunlight onto an elevated stationary linear receiver. - Receiver: Suspended above the mirror field, containing multiple water/steam boiling tubes. - Operating Temperature: $250^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 380^\circ\text{C}$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 10 \text{ to } 40$. - Advantages: Lower capital cost, lower wind load on ground mirrors, and higher land-use factor compared to PTC.

3. Central Receiver / Solar Power Tower (CR) - Point Focus: - Geometry & Optics: Employs a vast circular or semi-circular field of hundreds or thousands of dual-axis tracking flat mirrors called heliostats. All heliostats continuously track the sun and reflect rays onto a single central receiver mounted atop a tall tower ($100 \text{ to } 250\text{ meters}$). - Heat Transfer Fluid: Molten nitrate salt ($60\% \text{ NaNO}_3 + 40\% \text{ KNO}_3$) is pumped up the tower, heated to $565^\circ\text{C}$, and stored in massive insulated hot-salt tanks, enabling 10 to 15 hours of full-load thermal energy storage (TES) for dispatchable baseload generation during nighttime. - Operating Temperature: $550^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 800^\circ\text{C}+$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 500 \text{ to } 1500$.

4. Parabolic Dish Stirling System (PDS) - Point Focus: - Geometry & Optics: A standalone dish-shaped paraboloid mirror that concentrates sunlight onto a focal point receiver mounted on the dish structure, guided by dual-axis tracking. - Power Cycle: A compact Stirling or Brayton engine is placed directly at the focal receiver, using hydrogen or helium working gas. - Operating Temperature: $700^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 900^\circ\text{C}$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 600 \text{ to } 3000$. - Status: Demonstrates the highest solar-to-electric conversion efficiency (over $31\%$), but lacks thermal storage integration and has higher mechanical complexity per kW.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Concentrating Solar Power (CSP) systems utilize optical reflectors (mirrors) to concentrate direct normal solar irradiance (DNI) from a large aperture area onto a significantly smaller absorber target. Concentrating the solar flux achieves elevated working temperatures ($300^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 1000^\circ\text{C}+$ ), driving conventional steam or gas turbine thermal cycles at high thermodynamic efficiency.

Concentration Ratio ($C_R$): The fundamental figure of merit is the geometric concentration ratio $C_R$, defined as: $$C_R = \frac{\text{Aperture Area of Concentrator } (A_a)}{\text{Surface Area of Receiver Absorber } (A_r)}$$ As $C_R$ increases, the thermal loss area $A_r$ shrinks relative to the collecting aperture $A_a$, enabling much higher operating temperatures without excessive radiation heat loss.

1. Parabolic Trough Collector (PTC) - Line Focus: - Geometry & Optics: PTC consists of long, U-shaped parabolic curved mirrors that focus direct sunlight onto a linear metallic absorber tube positioned along the focal line. It utilizes single-axis tracking (typically tracking from East to West). - Receiver: A stainless steel pipe coated with cermet selective coating surrounded by an evacuated glass envelope to eliminate convection losses. Synthetic thermal oil or molten salt circulates through the tube. - Operating Temperature: $350^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 400^\circ\text{C}$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 15 \text{ to } 80$. - Status: The most mature and widely deployed CSP technology globally (e.g., SEGS and Mojave plants).

2. Linear Fresnel Reflector (LFR) - Line Focus: - Geometry & Optics: Instead of curved parabolic mirrors, LFR employs long, flat or slightly curved elastic mirror strips placed close to the ground that rotate independently to focus sunlight onto an elevated stationary linear receiver. - Receiver: Suspended above the mirror field, containing multiple water/steam boiling tubes. - Operating Temperature: $250^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 380^\circ\text{C}$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 10 \text{ to } 40$. - Advantages: Lower capital cost, lower wind load on ground mirrors, and higher land-use factor compared to PTC.

3. Central Receiver / Solar Power Tower (CR) - Point Focus: - Geometry & Optics: Employs a vast circular or semi-circular field of hundreds or thousands of dual-axis tracking flat mirrors called heliostats. All heliostats continuously track the sun and reflect rays onto a single central receiver mounted atop a tall tower ($100 \text{ to } 250\text{ meters}$). - Heat Transfer Fluid: Molten nitrate salt ($60\% \text{ NaNO}_3 + 40\% \text{ KNO}_3$) is pumped up the tower, heated to $565^\circ\text{C}$, and stored in massive insulated hot-salt tanks, enabling 10 to 15 hours of full-load thermal energy storage (TES) for dispatchable baseload generation during nighttime. - Operating Temperature: $550^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 800^\circ\text{C}+$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 500 \text{ to } 1500$.

4. Parabolic Dish Stirling System (PDS) - Point Focus: - Geometry & Optics: A standalone dish-shaped paraboloid mirror that concentrates sunlight onto a focal point receiver mounted on the dish structure, guided by dual-axis tracking. - Power Cycle: A compact Stirling or Brayton engine is placed directly at the focal receiver, using hydrogen or helium working gas. - Operating Temperature: $700^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 900^\circ\text{C}$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 600 \text{ to } 3000$. - Status: Demonstrates the highest solar-to-electric conversion efficiency (over $31\%$), but lacks thermal storage integration and has higher mechanical complexity per kW.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Concentrating Solar Power (CSP) systems utilize optical reflectors (mirrors) to concentrate direct normal solar irradiance (DNI) from a large aperture area onto a significantly smaller absorber target. Concentrating the solar flux achieves elevated working temperatures ($300^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 1000^\circ\text{C}+$ ), driving conventional steam or gas turbine thermal cycles at high thermodynamic efficiency.

Concentration Ratio ($C_R$): The fundamental figure of merit is the geometric concentration ratio $C_R$, defined as: $$C_R = \frac{\text{Aperture Area of Concentrator } (A_a)}{\text{Surface Area of Receiver Absorber } (A_r)}$$ As $C_R$ increases, the thermal loss area $A_r$ shrinks relative to the collecting aperture $A_a$, enabling much higher operating temperatures without excessive radiation heat loss.

1. Parabolic Trough Collector (PTC) - Line Focus: - Geometry & Optics: PTC consists of long, U-shaped parabolic curved mirrors that focus direct sunlight onto a linear metallic absorber tube positioned along the focal line. It utilizes single-axis tracking (typically tracking from East to West). - Receiver: A stainless steel pipe coated with cermet selective coating surrounded by an evacuated glass envelope to eliminate convection losses. Synthetic thermal oil or molten salt circulates through the tube. - Operating Temperature: $350^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 400^\circ\text{C}$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 15 \text{ to } 80$. - Status: The most mature and widely deployed CSP technology globally (e.g., SEGS and Mojave plants).

2. Linear Fresnel Reflector (LFR) - Line Focus: - Geometry & Optics: Instead of curved parabolic mirrors, LFR employs long, flat or slightly curved elastic mirror strips placed close to the ground that rotate independently to focus sunlight onto an elevated stationary linear receiver. - Receiver: Suspended above the mirror field, containing multiple water/steam boiling tubes. - Operating Temperature: $250^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 380^\circ\text{C}$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 10 \text{ to } 40$. - Advantages: Lower capital cost, lower wind load on ground mirrors, and higher land-use factor compared to PTC.

3. Central Receiver / Solar Power Tower (CR) - Point Focus: - Geometry & Optics: Employs a vast circular or semi-circular field of hundreds or thousands of dual-axis tracking flat mirrors called heliostats. All heliostats continuously track the sun and reflect rays onto a single central receiver mounted atop a tall tower ($100 \text{ to } 250\text{ meters}$). - Heat Transfer Fluid: Molten nitrate salt ($60\% \text{ NaNO}_3 + 40\% \text{ KNO}_3$) is pumped up the tower, heated to $565^\circ\text{C}$, and stored in massive insulated hot-salt tanks, enabling 10 to 15 hours of full-load thermal energy storage (TES) for dispatchable baseload generation during nighttime. - Operating Temperature: $550^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 800^\circ\text{C}+$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 500 \text{ to } 1500$.

4. Parabolic Dish Stirling System (PDS) - Point Focus: - Geometry & Optics: A standalone dish-shaped paraboloid mirror that concentrates sunlight onto a focal point receiver mounted on the dish structure, guided by dual-axis tracking. - Power Cycle: A compact Stirling or Brayton engine is placed directly at the focal receiver, using hydrogen or helium working gas. - Operating Temperature: $700^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 900^\circ\text{C}$; Concentration ratio $C_R \approx 600 \text{ to } 3000$. - Status: Demonstrates the highest solar-to-electric conversion efficiency (over $31\%$), but lacks thermal storage integration and has higher mechanical complexity per kW.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Descriptive ID: RES-Q8

Explain the effects of partial shading on PV module I-V and P-V characteristics. Discuss hot-spot formation and the protective role of bypass and blocking diodes. Explain the effects of partial shading on PV module I-V and P-V characteristics. Discuss hot-spot formation and the protective role of bypass and blocking diodes. Explain the effects of partial shading on PV module I-V and P-V characteristics. Discuss hot-spot formation and the protective role of bypass and blocking diodes.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$P_{dissipated} = |V_{reverse}| \times I_{string}, \quad V_{bypass} \approx -0.7\text{ V}$$

Hot-Spot Phenomenon: Shaded cell in series carries illuminated string current, forced into reverse bias and dissipating power as concentrated heat.
Bypass Diodes: Placed anti-parallel across cell groups to conduct string current when reverse-biased, protecting cells from burn-out.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$P_{dissipated} = |V_{reverse}| \times I_{string}, \quad V_{bypass} \approx -0.7\text{ V}$$

Hot-Spot Phenomenon: Shaded cell in series carries illuminated string current, forced into reverse bias and dissipating power as concentrated heat.
Bypass Diodes: Placed anti-parallel across cell groups to conduct string current when reverse-biased, protecting cells from burn-out.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$P_{dissipated} = |V_{reverse}| \times I_{string}, \quad V_{bypass} \approx -0.7\text{ V}$$

Hot-Spot Phenomenon: Shaded cell in series carries illuminated string current, forced into reverse bias and dissipating power as concentrated heat.
Bypass Diodes: Placed anti-parallel across cell groups to conduct string current when reverse-biased, protecting cells from burn-out.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

To generate utility-level voltages and power outputs, individual photovoltaic cells (which typically produce only $\approx 0.5 \text{ to } 0.6\text{ V}$ and $5 \text{ to } 10\text{ A}$) are interconnected into solar modules, strings, and multi-string arrays.

Series and Parallel Interconnections: - In a series string of $N_s$ cells, the total output voltage is the algebraic sum of the individual cell voltages ($V_{string} = \sum_{i=1}^{N_s} V_i$), while the current is strictly limited to the current of the weakest cell ($I_{string} = \min\{I_1, I_2, \dots, I_{N_s}\}$). - In a parallel grouping of $N_p$ strings, the total output current is the sum of the individual string currents ($I_{array} = \sum_{j=1}^{N_p} I_j$), while the array voltage equals the string voltage ($V_{array} = V_{string}$).

Partial Shading and Mismatch Phenomena: When a portion of a PV array is shaded by building obstructions, overhead transmission lines, tree foliage, bird droppings, or localized cloud cover, the illuminated irradiance on the shaded cells drops significantly. Because the short-circuit current of a PV cell is directly proportional to irradiance ($I_{sc} \propto G$), the shaded cell can produce only a reduced photocurrent. However, because all cells in the series string are forced to carry the higher current generated by the fully illuminated cells, the shaded cell is driven into reverse-bias operation ($V_{cell} < 0$). Under deep reverse bias, the shaded cell stops producing power and instead behaves as a resistive sink, dissipating power internally equal to: $$P_{dissipated} = |V_{reverse}| \times I_{string}$$ This localized electrical power dissipation is converted into intense heat. The localized temperature can exceed $150^\circ\text{C} \text{ to } 200^\circ\text{C}$, resulting in the destructive phenomenon known as Hot-Spot Formation. Hot spots cause localized EVA browning, backsheet melting, solder fatigue, glass cracking, and permanent cell burnout.

Role of Bypass Diodes: To protect series strings against hot-spot destruction, Bypass Diodes are connected in anti-parallel (reverse polarity) across groups of series cells (typically across 18 to 24 cells in standard 60-cell or 72-cell silicon modules). - Under normal uniform sunlight: All cells generate positive forward voltage. The bypass diode is reverse-biased and remains in an open-circuit state, consuming negligible leakage current. - Under partial shading: As the shaded cell becomes reverse-biased, the total net voltage across that sub-string drops below zero. When this reverse voltage reaches approximately $-0.7\text{ V}$, the bypass diode turns ON (forward-biased). The excess string current safely bypasses the shaded cell group through the forward-conducting diode, clamping the reverse voltage to approximately $-0.7\text{ V}$ and eliminating destructive hot-spot power dissipation. Consequence on P-V Characteristics: The activation of bypass diodes distorts the smooth, unimodal Power-Voltage curve, creating Multiple Local Maxima (LMPPs) and a single Global Maximum Power Point (GMPP). Conventional gradient-based MPPT algorithms fail by becoming trapped in local peaks, requiring advanced global tracking techniques.

Role of Blocking Diodes: Blocking Diodes are connected in series with each parallel string in an array or between the PV array and a battery storage bank: 1. They prevent reverse current from flowing from fully illuminated parallel strings into lower-voltage shaded strings. 2. In off-grid battery systems, they prevent the battery from discharging backward into the PV array during nighttime when the PV terminal voltage drops to zero.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: RES-Q9

Explain the Maximum Power Point Tracking (MPPT) principle. Derive the mathematical formulation of Perturb & Observe (P&O) and Incremental Conductance algorithms. Explain the Maximum Power Point Tracking (MPPT) principle. Derive the mathematical formulation of Perturb & Observe (P&O) and Incremental Conductance algorithms. Explain the Maximum Power Point Tracking (MPPT) principle. Derive the mathematical formulation of Perturb & Observe (P&O) and Incremental Conductance algorithms.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{dP}{dV} = 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} = -\frac{I}{V} \quad (\text{at MPP}), \quad \frac{dI}{dV} > -\frac{I}{V} \ (\text{Left}), \quad \frac{dI}{dV} < -\frac{I}{V} \ (\text{Right})$$

P&O MPPT: Perturbs voltage and observes power change; hunts in 3-point cycle.
Incremental Conductance: Tracks derivative $\frac{dI}{dV} + \frac{I}{V} = 0$, halting perturbations at exact peak and eliminating steady-state oscillation.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{dP}{dV} = 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} = -\frac{I}{V} \quad (\text{at MPP}), \quad \frac{dI}{dV} > -\frac{I}{V} \ (\text{Left}), \quad \frac{dI}{dV} < -\frac{I}{V} \ (\text{Right})$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{dP}{dV} = 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} = -\frac{I}{V} \quad (\text{at MPP}), \quad \frac{dI}{dV} > -\frac{I}{V} \ (\text{Left}), \quad \frac{dI}{dV} < -\frac{I}{V} \ (\text{Right})$$

P&O MPPT: Perturbs voltage and observes power change; hunts in 3-point cycle.
Incremental Conductance: Tracks derivative $\frac{dI}{dV} + \frac{I}{V} = 0$, halting perturbations at exact peak and eliminating steady-state oscillation.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Because the Power-Voltage ($P-V$) characteristic of a photovoltaic array is highly non-linear and continuously varies with solar irradiance and ambient temperature, Maximum Power Point Tracking (MPPT) is incorporated into power electronic interfaces (DC-DC buck, boost, or inverter stages) to continuously operate the array at its optimal voltage $V_{mp}$ and extract the maximum available power $P_{mp}$.

1. Perturb and Observe (P&O) Algorithm: Principle: P&O operates by periodically perturbing (incrementing or decrementing) the array operating voltage (or duty cycle $D$ of the DC-DC converter) and observing the resulting change in array output power. Mathematical Decision Logic: Let $V(k)$ and $I(k)$ be the measured voltage and current at iteration step $k$. The power is $P(k) = V(k) \cdot I(k)$. The changes in voltage and power between successive samples are: $$\Delta V = V(k) - V(k-1), \quad \Delta P = P(k) - P(k-1)$$ The control rules are: - If $\Delta P > 0$ and $\Delta V > 0$: Power is increasing; the operating point is moving up the left side of the $P-V$ curve toward the MPP. Therefore, continue perturbing in the same direction ($\Delta V > 0$, increase $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta P > 0$ and $\Delta V < 0$: Power is increasing with decreasing voltage; continue perturbing downward ($\Delta V < 0$, decrease $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta P < 0$ and $\Delta V > 0$: Power has decreased after increasing voltage; the operating point has overshot the peak and moved onto the right side of the MPP. Therefore, reverse the perturbation direction ($\Delta V < 0$, decrease $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta P < 0$ and $\Delta V < 0$: Power has decreased after decreasing voltage; reverse the perturbation direction ($\Delta V > 0$, increase $V_{ref}$).

Limitations of P&O: 1. Steady-State Oscillation: Around the MPP, the operating point continuously oscillates around $V_{mp}$ in a three-point limit cycle, causing steady-state power loss. 2. Divergence during Rapid Irradiance Shifts: If solar irradiance suddenly increases between sample steps, $\Delta P > 0$ even if the voltage perturbation moved away from the MPP, causing the algorithm to track in the wrong direction.

2. Incremental Conductance (IncCond) Algorithm: Derivation: The Incremental Conductance method eliminates the tracking errors of P&O by utilizing the exact mathematical derivative of the $P-V$ curve. The electrical power delivered by the PV array is: $$P = V \cdot I$$ Differentiating both sides with respect to terminal voltage $V$: $$\frac{dP}{dV} = \frac{d(V \cdot I)}{dV} = I \frac{dV}{dV} + V \frac{dI}{dV} = I + V \frac{dI}{dV}$$ Dividing through by terminal voltage $V$ ($V > 0$): $$\frac{1}{V} \frac{dP}{dV} = \frac{I}{V} + \frac{dI}{dV}$$ At the Maximum Power Point (MPP), the slope of the $P-V$ curve is zero: $$\frac{dP}{dV} = 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} = -\frac{I}{V}$$ To the left of the MPP (where power increases with voltage): $$\frac{dP}{dV} > 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} > -\frac{I}{V}$$ To the right of the MPP (where power decreases with voltage): $$\frac{dP}{dV} < 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} < -\frac{I}{V}$$ Here: - $\frac{I}{V}$ is the Instantaneous Conductance ($G$), - $\frac{dI}{dV} \approx \frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V}$ is the Incremental Conductance ($\Delta G$).

IncCond Control Logic: The controller measures instantaneous $V(k)$ and $I(k)$, computes $\Delta V = V(k) - V(k-1)$ and $\Delta I = I(k) - I(k-1)$: - If $\Delta V = 0$: Check if $\Delta I = 0$. If $\Delta I = 0$, the operating point is at the MPP. If $\Delta I > 0$, irradiance has increased (increase $V_{ref}$); if $\Delta I < 0$, irradiance has decreased (decrease $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta V \ne 0$: Compare $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V}$ with $-\frac{I}{V}$: - If $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V} = -\frac{I}{V}$: The system is exactly at the MPP; no voltage adjustment is needed. - If $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V} > -\frac{I}{V}$: The operating point is to the left of the MPP; increment reference voltage ($V_{ref} \leftarrow V_{ref} + \Delta V_{step}$). - If $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V} < -\frac{I}{V}$: The operating point is to the right of the MPP; decrement reference voltage ($V_{ref} \leftarrow V_{ref} - \Delta V_{step}$).

Advantages: The IncCond algorithm can recognize when the array reaches the MPP and halt perturbations, completely eliminating steady-state limit-cycle hunting and accurately tracking dynamic atmospheric variations.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Because the Power-Voltage ($P-V$) characteristic of a photovoltaic array is highly non-linear and continuously varies with solar irradiance and ambient temperature, Maximum Power Point Tracking (MPPT) is incorporated into power electronic interfaces (DC-DC buck, boost, or inverter stages) to continuously operate the array at its optimal voltage $V_{mp}$ and extract the maximum available power $P_{mp}$.

1. Perturb and Observe (P&O) Algorithm: Principle: P&O operates by periodically perturbing (incrementing or decrementing) the array operating voltage (or duty cycle $D$ of the DC-DC converter) and observing the resulting change in array output power. Mathematical Decision Logic: Let $V(k)$ and $I(k)$ be the measured voltage and current at iteration step $k$. The power is $P(k) = V(k) \cdot I(k)$. The changes in voltage and power between successive samples are: $$\Delta V = V(k) - V(k-1), \quad \Delta P = P(k) - P(k-1)$$ The control rules are: - If $\Delta P > 0$ and $\Delta V > 0$: Power is increasing; the operating point is moving up the left side of the $P-V$ curve toward the MPP. Therefore, continue perturbing in the same direction ($\Delta V > 0$, increase $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta P > 0$ and $\Delta V < 0$: Power is increasing with decreasing voltage; continue perturbing downward ($\Delta V < 0$, decrease $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta P < 0$ and $\Delta V > 0$: Power has decreased after increasing voltage; the operating point has overshot the peak and moved onto the right side of the MPP. Therefore, reverse the perturbation direction ($\Delta V < 0$, decrease $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta P < 0$ and $\Delta V < 0$: Power has decreased after decreasing voltage; reverse the perturbation direction ($\Delta V > 0$, increase $V_{ref}$).

Limitations of P&O: 1. Steady-State Oscillation: Around the MPP, the operating point continuously oscillates around $V_{mp}$ in a three-point limit cycle, causing steady-state power loss. 2. Divergence during Rapid Irradiance Shifts: If solar irradiance suddenly increases between sample steps, $\Delta P > 0$ even if the voltage perturbation moved away from the MPP, causing the algorithm to track in the wrong direction.

2. Incremental Conductance (IncCond) Algorithm: Derivation: The Incremental Conductance method eliminates the tracking errors of P&O by utilizing the exact mathematical derivative of the $P-V$ curve. The electrical power delivered by the PV array is: $$P = V \cdot I$$ Differentiating both sides with respect to terminal voltage $V$: $$\frac{dP}{dV} = \frac{d(V \cdot I)}{dV} = I \frac{dV}{dV} + V \frac{dI}{dV} = I + V \frac{dI}{dV}$$ Dividing through by terminal voltage $V$ ($V > 0$): $$\frac{1}{V} \frac{dP}{dV} = \frac{I}{V} + \frac{dI}{dV}$$ At the Maximum Power Point (MPP), the slope of the $P-V$ curve is zero: $$\frac{dP}{dV} = 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} = -\frac{I}{V}$$ To the left of the MPP (where power increases with voltage): $$\frac{dP}{dV} > 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} > -\frac{I}{V}$$ To the right of the MPP (where power decreases with voltage): $$\frac{dP}{dV} < 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} < -\frac{I}{V}$$ Here: - $\frac{I}{V}$ is the Instantaneous Conductance ($G$), - $\frac{dI}{dV} \approx \frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V}$ is the Incremental Conductance ($\Delta G$).

IncCond Control Logic: The controller measures instantaneous $V(k)$ and $I(k)$, computes $\Delta V = V(k) - V(k-1)$ and $\Delta I = I(k) - I(k-1)$: - If $\Delta V = 0$: Check if $\Delta I = 0$. If $\Delta I = 0$, the operating point is at the MPP. If $\Delta I > 0$, irradiance has increased (increase $V_{ref}$); if $\Delta I < 0$, irradiance has decreased (decrease $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta V \ne 0$: Compare $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V}$ with $-\frac{I}{V}$: - If $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V} = -\frac{I}{V}$: The system is exactly at the MPP; no voltage adjustment is needed. - If $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V} > -\frac{I}{V}$: The operating point is to the left of the MPP; increment reference voltage ($V_{ref} \leftarrow V_{ref} + \Delta V_{step}$). - If $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V} < -\frac{I}{V}$: The operating point is to the right of the MPP; decrement reference voltage ($V_{ref} \leftarrow V_{ref} - \Delta V_{step}$).

Advantages: The IncCond algorithm can recognize when the array reaches the MPP and halt perturbations, completely eliminating steady-state limit-cycle hunting and accurately tracking dynamic atmospheric variations.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Because the Power-Voltage ($P-V$) characteristic of a photovoltaic array is highly non-linear and continuously varies with solar irradiance and ambient temperature, Maximum Power Point Tracking (MPPT) is incorporated into power electronic interfaces (DC-DC buck, boost, or inverter stages) to continuously operate the array at its optimal voltage $V_{mp}$ and extract the maximum available power $P_{mp}$.

1. Perturb and Observe (P&O) Algorithm: Principle: P&O operates by periodically perturbing (incrementing or decrementing) the array operating voltage (or duty cycle $D$ of the DC-DC converter) and observing the resulting change in array output power. Mathematical Decision Logic: Let $V(k)$ and $I(k)$ be the measured voltage and current at iteration step $k$. The power is $P(k) = V(k) \cdot I(k)$. The changes in voltage and power between successive samples are: $$\Delta V = V(k) - V(k-1), \quad \Delta P = P(k) - P(k-1)$$ The control rules are: - If $\Delta P > 0$ and $\Delta V > 0$: Power is increasing; the operating point is moving up the left side of the $P-V$ curve toward the MPP. Therefore, continue perturbing in the same direction ($\Delta V > 0$, increase $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta P > 0$ and $\Delta V < 0$: Power is increasing with decreasing voltage; continue perturbing downward ($\Delta V < 0$, decrease $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta P < 0$ and $\Delta V > 0$: Power has decreased after increasing voltage; the operating point has overshot the peak and moved onto the right side of the MPP. Therefore, reverse the perturbation direction ($\Delta V < 0$, decrease $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta P < 0$ and $\Delta V < 0$: Power has decreased after decreasing voltage; reverse the perturbation direction ($\Delta V > 0$, increase $V_{ref}$).

Limitations of P&O: 1. Steady-State Oscillation: Around the MPP, the operating point continuously oscillates around $V_{mp}$ in a three-point limit cycle, causing steady-state power loss. 2. Divergence during Rapid Irradiance Shifts: If solar irradiance suddenly increases between sample steps, $\Delta P > 0$ even if the voltage perturbation moved away from the MPP, causing the algorithm to track in the wrong direction.

2. Incremental Conductance (IncCond) Algorithm: Derivation: The Incremental Conductance method eliminates the tracking errors of P&O by utilizing the exact mathematical derivative of the $P-V$ curve. The electrical power delivered by the PV array is: $$P = V \cdot I$$ Differentiating both sides with respect to terminal voltage $V$: $$\frac{dP}{dV} = \frac{d(V \cdot I)}{dV} = I \frac{dV}{dV} + V \frac{dI}{dV} = I + V \frac{dI}{dV}$$ Dividing through by terminal voltage $V$ ($V > 0$): $$\frac{1}{V} \frac{dP}{dV} = \frac{I}{V} + \frac{dI}{dV}$$ At the Maximum Power Point (MPP), the slope of the $P-V$ curve is zero: $$\frac{dP}{dV} = 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} = -\frac{I}{V}$$ To the left of the MPP (where power increases with voltage): $$\frac{dP}{dV} > 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} > -\frac{I}{V}$$ To the right of the MPP (where power decreases with voltage): $$\frac{dP}{dV} < 0 \implies \frac{dI}{dV} < -\frac{I}{V}$$ Here: - $\frac{I}{V}$ is the Instantaneous Conductance ($G$), - $\frac{dI}{dV} \approx \frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V}$ is the Incremental Conductance ($\Delta G$).

IncCond Control Logic: The controller measures instantaneous $V(k)$ and $I(k)$, computes $\Delta V = V(k) - V(k-1)$ and $\Delta I = I(k) - I(k-1)$: - If $\Delta V = 0$: Check if $\Delta I = 0$. If $\Delta I = 0$, the operating point is at the MPP. If $\Delta I > 0$, irradiance has increased (increase $V_{ref}$); if $\Delta I < 0$, irradiance has decreased (decrease $V_{ref}$). - If $\Delta V \ne 0$: Compare $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V}$ with $-\frac{I}{V}$: - If $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V} = -\frac{I}{V}$: The system is exactly at the MPP; no voltage adjustment is needed. - If $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V} > -\frac{I}{V}$: The operating point is to the left of the MPP; increment reference voltage ($V_{ref} \leftarrow V_{ref} + \Delta V_{step}$). - If $\frac{\Delta I}{\Delta V} < -\frac{I}{V}$: The operating point is to the right of the MPP; decrement reference voltage ($V_{ref} \leftarrow V_{ref} - \Delta V_{step}$).

Advantages: The IncCond algorithm can recognize when the array reaches the MPP and halt perturbations, completely eliminating steady-state limit-cycle hunting and accurately tracking dynamic atmospheric variations.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 2.4: Flowcharts and Mathematical Derivatives for P&O and Incremental Conductance MPPT Methods — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)

Descriptive ID: RES-Q10

Compare grid-tied and stand-alone solar PV systems with functional block diagrams, balance of system (BOS) components, and inverter configurations. Compare grid-tied and stand-alone solar PV systems with functional block diagrams, balance of system (BOS) components, and inverter configurations. Compare grid-tied and stand-alone solar PV systems with functional block diagrams, balance of system (BOS) components, and inverter configurations.

Stand-Alone PV: Battery storage bank, MPPT charge controller, and off-grid inverter operate autonomously.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$P_{import} = P_{load} - P_{pv}, \quad t_{islanding} \le 2.0\text{ s}$$

Stand-Alone PV: Battery storage bank, MPPT charge controller, and off-grid inverter operate autonomously.
